But I still check.\n\nAnd if I looked bigger there, even a little, I don’t think I’d feel satisfied. It would set me off for the day. Like I’d be thinking about my shirt, my belt, how I’m standing… and whether I’d let myself slip. It’s hard to respect myself when I don’t look lean enough to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain physical appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s probably more about how I look, if I’m being honest… though the weight number gets wrapped up in it.\n\nLike, if I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people keep saying they see. I’m not standing there thinking, “Oh, I’m fine.” I go straight to my stomach, my waist, whether I look lean enough. And then that decides how I feel about myself that day, which sounds dramatic, but it’s true.\n\nI remember in the locker room at work, you know, the lighting in there is awful—those old mirrors make everybody look strange—and I’ll still check. Even if I’m supposed to be getting ready or heading out, I’m looking at my belt line, my shirt, whether anything looks soft. And if it does, I feel like I’ve lost respect for myself somehow.\n\nSo… yeah. Avoiding weight gain is part of it, but it’s really about not looking bigger there. Not letting myself look like I’ve slipped.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you've lost control over your life in general?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does feel that way. Not in some big dramatic way, like my whole life is falling apart, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this… jolt. Like something slipped past me.\n\nAnd then I’m thinking about it all day. I’ll start cutting back harder, like I have to correct it right away, because I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nIt’s strange, because at work you’re trained to stay calm, keep things contained, follow procedure—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. But that’s kind of how it feels. If that number goes up, even a little, it feels like I didn’t keep control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as overweight even if other people told you that you are at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. I mean, people already say things like that, that I’m “fine” or whatever, and I don’t really… it doesn’t land.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I’m not seeing the whole picture the way they apparently are. I go right to my stomach, my waist, whether I look lean enough. It’s like everything else disappears. Even if someone tells me I’m a healthy weight, I’m still thinking, “But do I look bigger there?”\n\nAnd then that kind of decides the day, which I know sounds stupid. Like if my waist looks off, I’m uncomfortable in my clothes, I’m thinking about how I’m standing, if my shirt is sitting wrong… I had this old uniform shirt once that never fit right, and I swear it could ruin my mood before a shift even started. Sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, I’d still see something wrong. I’d still feel like I looked too big, or not lean enough to respect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your own weight is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it must be. I mean, they’ll say I look fine, or that I’m not seeing myself clearly, but when I look in the mirror, I don’t see what they’re talking about.\n\nI go right to my stomach and my waist. Like, I’m not looking at myself as a whole person, I’m checking whether I look lean enough. And if I don’t, then that sort of colors the entire day. I’ll be thinking about how my shirt sits or whether my belt feels different—sorry, that’s probably more detail than you need.\n\nBut yes. Other people seem to see something different than I do. To me it feels obvious, like if I look too soft there, then I’ve let myself go somehow. Like I can’t really respect myself that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if a doctor told you that your weight is healthy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. Even if a doctor said the number was healthy, I don’t know that I’d believe it in the way I’m supposed to.\n\nBecause when I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else says they see. I go straight to my stomach and my waist, and I’m measuring the whole day off that, almost. Whether I look lean enough, whether I can… I don’t know, respect myself when I see it.\n\nIt’s like with inspections, you can have someone say everything’s within standard, but if you notice one thing off, that’s all you see. Sorry, that’s probably not the best example. But yes, I’d still want to lose weight if I felt I looked too big there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake even if you reached your desired body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, I want to say if I got to the right shape I’d ease up, but I don’t really know that I would.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is occupational health saying I’m not eating enough. And they’re probably right. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, or a few bites of chicken at night. Not a real meal.\n\nAnd the thing is, even if I looked the way I wanted, a full meal would still feel like… like I was letting my standards slip. Like once you loosen up, that’s when things start going bad. That happened on the job too, you know, with fitness tests and uniforms and everyone pretending they don’t notice who’s getting soft—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nBut yes. I think I’d still restrict it. Because eating normally doesn’t feel normal to me. It feels like giving in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like restricting your food intake is more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. The control part is there, but if I’m honest, the thing that hits me hardest is the idea of gaining.\n\nLike if I step on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I get this… jolt, almost. Like I’ve messed up or lost control somehow. And then the whole day changes. I’ll cut back harder, tell myself I need to tighten things up.\n\nIt probably sounds ridiculous, half a pound. I know scales shift, water weight, all that. We used to joke about guys weighing in before fitness checks like it was some big strategy thing—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on. So restricting feels like the way I keep it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to restrict your food intake more about preventing weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control over your body?\n\nPatient: More preventing weight gain, I think. The control is part of it, sure, but the thing that really sets me off is the scale going up.\n\nEven if it’s just half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve failed or lost control of myself. Then I’m thinking about it all day, cutting back harder, trying to fix it before it gets worse.\n\nIt’s funny—not funny, but—on the job you get used to watching small changes because small things turn into bigger problems if you ignore them. But with this, I know half a pound probably shouldn’t mean anything. It still does to me. I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as needing to lose weight even if you were at a weight that is considered healthy by medical standards?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would.\n\nI mean, people can say “healthy weight” or whatever the chart says, but when I look in the mirror, I don’t see that. I don’t see what they say they’re seeing. My eyes go straight to my stomach, my waist… whether there’s anything soft there, whether I look lean enough.\n\nIt’s like the rest of me doesn’t count. I can have a whole day going fine, and then I catch myself in the mirror and if my waist looks wrong, that’s it. I feel like I don’t respect myself. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nUniforms make it worse too—well, not just uniforms, even regular clothes, the belt line, how things sit. Sorry, I’m wandering. But yes, even at a medically healthy weight, I’d probably still think I needed to lose more if I didn’t look right to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fear is because you think you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly “unattractive.” That’s part of it, maybe, but it feels more immediate than that.\n\nIf the scale is up even half a pound, I get this shock through me, like I’ve lost control of things. Like I let something slip that I should’ve been watching. And then the whole day becomes about correcting it—cutting back harder, being stricter.\n\nI mean, in police work you learn to pay attention to small changes, because sometimes that’s what tells you something’s going wrong—sorry, I know that’s probably not what you asked. But it feels like that in my head. Half a pound turns into this warning sign, and I can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on. So yeah, maybe looking worse is in there, but the bigger thing is just… the gain itself.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control of your body or about how you think you would look if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: It’s more the losing control part, I think. The way I look matters, sure, but the panic comes before I even get that far.\n\nIf the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like something’s slipped and I didn’t catch it. Then I’m thinking about it all day, trying to correct it, cutting back harder.\n\nIt’s like—sorry, this is probably a work habit too—you learn to notice small changes before they turn into bigger problems. But with my body, I take that too far. Half a pound feels like a warning light. I just can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

      "onset of symptoms prior to age 18 not explicitly confirmed, but patient's symptoms appear to be long-standing",
      "patient's symptoms are not solely explained by a lack of knowledge about nutrition or healthy eating"
    ]
  }
}
